Hyperliquid Pushes for 7 Key Tweaks Ahead of UK’s 2027 Crypto Overhaul

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

The Hyperliquid Policy Center (HPC) sent a letter to the UK Financial Conduct Authority (FCA) on Nov. 18 with seven recommendations on the draft documents CP26/36 and GC26/7. Three of them concern CP26/36, and four concern GC26/7. The letter was signed by HPC chief executive Jake Chervinsky.

The UK's new crypto regime will start to apply on Oct. 25, 2027. The FCA opened its window for crypto license applications on Sept. 30, 2026, and the window will close on Feb. 28, 2027.

According to the letter, "Regulatory obligations belong with persons who have unilateral control over a function that is a regulated activity." HPC argued that attaching obligations to a person who could not discharge them "would offer consumers no additional protection."

In the letter, HPC also compared public blockchains with the internet. "Like a public blockchain, the internet depends on protocols that identifiable persons maintain and upgrade over time," the group wrote.

HPC asked the FCA to assess each participant in a blockchain network separately. Proprietary traders and market makers "supply much of the liquidity on which order books depend," the group wrote. HPC also asked the regulator to describe compliance requirements as outcomes and to hold regulated firms accountable for them.

On Aug. 24, HPC urged the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) to harmonize their rules for perpetual contracts. The group argued that such products should be classified by their economic structure. According to HPC, a harmonized framework would let exchanges compete on "execution quality and liquidity."

On Sept. 30, HPC sent its response to the European Union's review of the MiCA rules. In it, the group asked the EU to treat crypto perpetual futures as derivatives under MiFID II. "Clarity in application, rather than new legislation, is what would assist in regulating perpetual futures," HPC wrote at the time.

Chervinsky previously served as chief policy officer at the Blockchain Association and as chief legal officer at the crypto venture firm Variant.

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Madden movie, starring Nicolas Cage as the legendary coach, broadcaster, and video-game mogul, has landed on Prime Video. In the eyes of critics, it has landed with a thud.

The current consensus at Rotten Tomatoes, based on 16 reviews, is 13 percent.

The folks at Sports Business Journal have collected and summarized some of the feedback. Owen Gleiberman of Variety calls it a “stunt biopic on cinematic amphetamines,” adding that “it’s like an SNL sketch shot and edited by Scorsese.”

Then there’s this incredible lede from David Rooney of the Hollywood Reporter: “Imagine being cornered at a party while sober by a wired cokehead determined to tell you a long, painstakingly detailed story without pausing for breath and you might have some idea of the exhausting experience of watching Madden.”

Bryan Curtis of The Ringer says "[i]t’s not quite as awful as the trailers made it look,” but that it still “isn’t good.” Curtis also says Christian Bale is “mesmerizing” as Al Davis, which raises the question of why they didn’t just make an Al Davis movie?

Of course, I’ll still watch it. You should, too. But I have a feeling that, throughout the experience, I’ll be wondering how much John Madden himself would have hated it.

Marco Materazzi Criticised For Comments About Jesus de Miguel’s Death

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

It's been just two weeks since the tragic death of CD Tenerife forward Jesus de Miguel, who passed away following an on-pitch collision with FC Andorra goalkeeper Jesus Owono.

The 30-year-old was rushed to hospital after the incident, which saw Owono's knee make contact with his chest. However, the Spanish Segunda Division club announced his passing the next morning.

Jesus Owono has broken his silence after the tragic passing of CD Tenerife forward Jesus de Miguel.

Owono has since been handed a 12-match ban and is contemplating whether he ever wants to return to football. The Spanish police opened an involuntary manslaughter investigation following the tragic events.

Marco Materazzi played just under 500 professional matches during his 25-year career. Of those appearances, 41 came for Italy.

His most significant moment in football was lifting the 2006 World Cup with his country. However, he's not best remembered for the goal he scored in the final against France or his overall contribution to the match itself.

Instead, the first thing that comes to mind for the majority of fans who hear his name is that Zinedine Zidane headbutt. Zizou reportedly took exception to a comment Materazzi made to him during the match and responded by planting his head into the Italian's chest, ending his playing career on a sour note.

Referee Horacio Elizondo has opened up on one of the most iconic moments in World Cup history.

Materazzi has been in the headlines again recently. This time, for comments he made relating to the death of De Miguel. The 53-year-old compared his physical collision with Zidane to the challenge that saw Di Miguel lose his life.

Speaking to Gazzetta dello Sport, he said: "The death of Jesús de Miguel made me thank God that nothing had happened. They're talking about manslaughter for a gambling tactic: what if it had happened to me?"

Those words have been criticised by football fans on social media and have been labelled 'tone-deaf'. One supporter said: "It's so tone-deaf," while another added: "How can I make this about me?"

Another questioned: "Is this not an insane thing to say? Seriously. How self-centred and tone-deaf can you be?" One Reddit user said: "Read the room, Marco."

Another World Cup winner who has given his thoughts on the situation is Gerard Pique. The former Spain and Barcelona defender is now the owner of FC Andorra, Owono's club.

Tenerife manager Alvaro Cervera has responded to the sanction given to Jesus Owono for his role in the incident that caused Jesus de Miguel's death.

Responding to the 12-game suspension handed out to the goalkeeper, Pique claimed the sanction placed blame on Owono. He said:

"Why did this happen? Players, former players, coaches, goalkeepers... they’ve all called it an accident. I truly believe that imposing the maximum penalty on Jesús for this incident puts him in a situation he doesn’t deserve, because he isn’t to blame for any of this. My take on all this is that the 12-game suspension for Jesús Owono seems to me to be placing the blame on Jesús for consequences that weren’t his fault. I believe the penalty should be based on the action itself, not on the consequences."

The Sound of Rocky: How an Anthem Was Born

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

How a 90-second musical cue for a low-budget boxing movie became an enduring anthem of perseverance inspiring generations far beyond the big screen.

When composer Bill Conti first sat down to write the music for Rocky’s now-iconic training montage, he wasn’t trying to create one of the most recognizable anthems in cinematic history.

Director John G. Avildsen simply needed 90 seconds of music to accompany Sylvester Stallone’s transformation from a struggling Philadelphia boxer into a legitimate heavyweight contender.

Conti took a melancholy theme he’d already developed for the film, gave it a more energetic tempo, and recorded a rough version for Avildsen to use in the editing room. Related Stories Movies How Rocky Became the Heart of Philadelphia Movies The Underdog Story Behind the Underdog Story

The director liked what he heard. But he needed more.

“He says, ‘I got five miles of film here. Give me another 30 seconds,’” Conti recalled. “I didn’t sit down to write a song. He needed a minute and a half. Then it grew to two, then two and a half and three.”

That unlikely creative process would eventually produce “Gonna Fly Now,” the triumphant theme that has accompanied Rocky Balboa’s ascent up the Philadelphia Museum of Art steps for half a century.

Like the film itself, the music was created with limited resources and even less room for error.

Conti had joined the production through editor Richard Halsey and Avildsen, accepting a $25,000 all-inclusive arrangement to compose and record the film’s score.

“A package deal means exactly what it says,” Conti said. “You pay for everything, and you get what’s left.”

To stretch the budget, Conti calculated the musical timings in advance, eliminating the need to project footage during recording sessions. But there was one element he wasn’t willing to compromise: six trumpet players to deliver the soaring fanfare that would become synonymous with Rocky’s determination.

“I saved the money in that we did the session real fast,” Conti said. “Clint Eastwood style, there was one take.”

While Conti was developing the music, lyricists Carol Connors and Ayn Robbins were tasked with finding the words to match its emotional intensity.

The right words initially eluded Connors. After watching an early version of the film and hearing Conti’s melody at her piano, she searched for lyrics that could capture Rocky’s journey without overwhelming the music.

The breakthrough came in an unlikely place: her shower.

“I know what the film’s about. I know what life’s about. I know what the song’s about,” Connors said.

There was no time to waste. Connors recalled grabbing her telephone while still in the shower and calling Conti to share her eureka moment.

With Rocky’s triumphant ascent up the museum steps in mind, the lyrics finally came together.

“He can go the distance,” Connors said. “He can even fly. And the song became ‘Gonna Fly Now.’”

Connors and Robbins ultimately wrote a complete set of lyrics, but the full words have never seen the light of day. Avildsen preferred to let the music carry the sequence, using only a handful of words rather than the full composition.

The decision gave the theme its distinctive simplicity, allowing Conti’s soaring instrumentation and the brief vocal refrain to communicate the emotion of Rocky’s transformation.

When Rocky arrived in theaters in 1976, audiences responded to the film’s unlikely hero and the music that accompanied his journey.

Among them was a teenage Peter Farrelly, who would go on to direct I Play Rocky, a film chronicling Stallone’s fight to bring the original movie to the screen.

“Boy, that music elevated it,” Farrelly remembered. “Without Bill Conti’s score, it’s not the same thing.”

The song’s impact soon extended beyond the movie theater. Released as a single, “Gonna Fly Now” reached No. 1 on the Billboard Hot 100, becoming a fixture of sporting events, training montages and moments of personal triumph.

Connors hadn’t anticipated just how far the song would travel.

“I would always relate it to the film, the film, the film,” Connors said. “But then all of a sudden, it dawned upon me that it had a life of its own.”

Over the ensuing decades, the anthem became more than a soundtrack for athletic achievement. Both Conti and Connors began hearing from people who had turned to the song during some of the most difficult moments of their lives.

“I did not realize how this song has helped people, has healed people, has made them go the distance,” Connors said.

Conti, too, recalled learning how the theme had inspired listeners facing their own personal battles, an impact he never could have anticipated when Avildsen first requested those 90 seconds of music.

“That it’s moved people is such an honor and such a blessing that I’m grateful every day for it,” Conti said.

Fifty years after its creation, “Gonna Fly Now” remains inseparable from the image of Rocky Balboa reaching the top of the museum steps. But its greatest legacy may be the countless people who have made the music part of their own journeys, finding strength in its message that perseverance can make the impossible feel within reach.

As Conti put it, “The future has hope. Unlike the past, the past is what it is. But the future always has hope.”

GLP-1 Eye Warning Leaves Patients, Doctors With a Difficult Choice

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

— FDA should study outcomes when patients stop, continue, or change treatment after NAION

When someone loses vision in one eye while taking a GLP-1 drug, the first priority is an urgent eye examination. If the diagnosis is non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION), the question that follows is: should we stop the drug?

On November 9, FDA announced it is requiring new warnings across all products in the GLP-1 drug class, which includes semaglutide (Ozempic, Wegovy) and tirzepatide (Zepbound, Mounjaro), among others, about a possible increased risk of NAION.

NAION occurs when inadequate blood flow damages the nerve that carries visual information from the eye to the brain. It can cause sudden, painless vision loss, often noticed on waking. The loss can be permanent, and there is no proven treatment to reverse it. The condition is rare, but these medications are widely used: in a March 2026 KFF poll, about one in eight U.S. adults reported currently taking a GLP-1 drug and one in five reported ever taking one.

As an ophthalmologist, I have routinely asked patients with suspected NAION about GLP-1 use for the past 2 years. I welcome the warning. The FDA should now pair it with a plan to study what happens when patients stop, continue, or change treatment after a NAION diagnosis.

People who develop NAION in one eye are already at increased risk of developing it in the other. How GLP-1 drugs might contribute remains uncertain. One hypothesis is that rapid reductions in blood sugar could cause swelling where the optic nerve enters the eye, compromising blood flow in susceptible patients. That possibility does not tell us whether continuing the medication adds to the risk in the other eye, or whether stopping reduces it.

These medications can be difficult to give up. Some patients take them to manage diabetes that has resisted other treatment. Certain drugs in the class also reduce the risk of heart attacks or worsening kidney disease in eligible patients. Stopping requires a plan for the condition being treated. When counseling patients who develop NAION while taking a GLP-1 drug, I recommend involving the prescribing doctor before changing treatment.

Regulators around the world have responded differently to this dilemma. European guidance says to stop semaglutide if NAION is confirmed. The FDA, whose warning covers the broader drug class, advises clinicians to consider discontinuation unless the benefits clearly outweigh the risk. I support that approach, with the patient, ophthalmologist, and prescribing doctor weighing the potential harm to vision against the benefits of treatment with a GLP-1.

Across 126 clinical trials, the FDA identified 15 probable NAION cases. The rates were equivalent to roughly six cases per 100,000 patients followed for a year among those receiving GLP-1 drugs, compared with four among those receiving a placebo or another diabetes medication. Larger observational studies have also raised concern, though their estimates vary and they cannot fully separate the effects of treatment from the conditions that led patients to receive it.

But estimating the risk of developing NAION does not tell us whether a patient should continue a GLP-1 drug after NAION diagnosis. One major FDA study excluded people with previous damage to the optic nerve from inadequate blood flow to help identify new cases accurately. That was a sensible design for its purpose, but it also means the study cannot establish whether continuing treatment after a diagnosis changes the risk to the other eye.

Some research has examined second-eye involvement. An analysis presented at the American Academy of Ophthalmology annual meeting this fall found that, among patients who developed NAION in both eyes, the second eye was affected sooner in GLP-1 users. It did not establish whether stopping would have prevented that outcome. Nor do we have reliable comparative evidence that switching to another GLP-1 drug is safer.

The FDA should assess whether its existing safety surveillance can answer these questions. If those systems fall short, the agency should require manufacturers to support additional studies. Federal law gives the agency that authority for serious safety concerns when routine reporting and available surveillance are insufficient.

The work could begin in participating eye clinics, linking confirmed diagnoses to prescribing and medical records. Researchers would document which medication a patient was taking, whether it was stopped or changed, when, and why. They would follow vision in the other eye alongside overall health, including how well replacement treatment managed diabetes or other conditions. Researchers would need to account for differences in diabetes severity and visual damage that affect both treatment decisions and outcomes.

The FDA should publish the study plans, identify who is responsible, and set dates for progress updates. Funding should be disclosed and results analyzed independently of manufacturers. Because NAION is uncommon, firm answers may take years. Patients and clinicians should still be able to see what is being learned and what remains uncertain.

The patient deciding what to do about the next dose cannot wait years for better evidence. Today's decision will have to be made with what we know. We owe that patient our best judgment, and we should follow what happens afterward. The next person facing the same choice deserves more to go on.

Your Bill Due Dates Aren’t Fixed – Move Them to Follow Your Paychecks

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

When your bills come due before the paycheck that covers them, ask to move the due dates. Most card issuers let you pick a new date online, and many lenders and utilities will do the same. Set each bill to land after the payday that covers it, and the late fees and overdrafts stop.

Say you’re paid every other Friday, and rent, the car payment, and a card bill all come due between the 1st and the 5th. One paycheck often has to carry all three, so a pricey week before the 1st can end in an overdraft or a late payment.

Pay that comes every two weeks follows a pattern you can plan around. Any 14 days in a row hold exactly one payday, so one check always lands between the 1st and the 14th and another between the 15th and the 28th.

List every bill with its due date and amount, then split them between two dates. Bills due early in the month draw on the check that landed between the 15th and the 28th of the month before. Bills due around the 17th draw on the one that lands by the 14th, at least three days ahead. Leave rent where your lease puts it and build the early group around it. In the example, the car payment and card bill move to the 17th, and each paycheck now carries its own share. If you’re paid on the 15th and the last day of the month, put the mid-month bills on the 18th, three days after that paycheck.

Start with your cards. They’re the easiest bills to move, and a card payment that reaches 30 days late generally goes on your credit report. Expect a billing cycle or two before the new date kicks in, and keep paying by the old one until a statement shows the change.

Car loans allow fewer moves than cards. Chase lets you change an auto loan’s due date twice over the life of the loan, and Wells Fargo just once. Many utilities run a preferred due date program, and some cap changes at one a year. Pick dates you can keep, then set each bill’s autopay for its new due date.

It’s an afternoon of clicks and calls, so do it this week. Then build a month of cushion in checking. Once it’s there, due dates stop mattering at all.

Bigfoot Is Back In GTA 6, And Apparently He Smells Revolting

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Grand Theft Auto games have been home to some weird phenomena over the years, and in GTA 6, it looks like Bigfoot is back and stinkier than ever. As the game steadily rolls out worldwide--or as people digitally immigrate to New Zealand for early access--streamers have been posting clips online of their encounters with the cryptid, and this time, it doesn't look like a man in a suit.

It looks like the Bigfoot sighting is tied to a mission in GTA 6, as the mysterious creature is wandering the woods of Leonida and it takes off in a panic when you encounter it. Another clip shows a strange encounter in the woods, with the player interacting with an effigy that emits noxious fumes and knocks them out. In a brief cutscene, Bigfoots, uh, really big feet, can be seen approaching Jason as he drifts in and out of consciousness.

In that first clip, Bigfoot is also covered in a cloud of fumes, which likely references stories about the mythical creature's potent stench. For GTA--and Red Dead Redemption--fans, this isn't the first time that they've been involved in a Bigfoot sighting, as encounters with it stretch back decades.

Back in 2004, rumors began to circulate about Bigfoot existing in GTA: San Andreas, but Rockstar dismissed these reports as false in 2005. A few years later, in Red Dead Redemption's Undead Nightmare expansion, protagonist John Marston was tasked with hunting down a pack of Sasquatches, and it was eventually revealed that the creatures were peaceful, capable of human speech, and now going extinct thanks to a certain trigger-happy cowboy.

Bigfoot would appear in GTA 5, as getting 100% completion in the game would unlock a new mission where co-protagonist Franklin hunts down a creature in Blaine Woods, only to discover it was a man in a costume. As an extra joke, the Bigfoot impersonator drops a few references to Red Dead Redemption's infamous encounter, but that doesn't mean the real Bigfoot isn't lurking in GTA 5. You could use the thermal scope on the sniper rifle supplied to you to spot a bipedal creature watching you during the "Predator" mission, and eating a golden Peyote plant under specific environmental conditions would turn players into Bigfoot.

Red Dead Redemption 2 brought an air of mystery back to the Bigfoot mythos, as you could find a gigantic skeleton half-buried in the snow high up on Mount Shann, and protagonist Arthur Morgan could also speak to an unseen figure that was heavily implied to be Bigfoot.

Meanwhile, you can check out our GTA 6 guides hub for more details on how to make the most out of your trip to Vice City.

What will OpenAI’s new outside safety evaluator tell the public?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Under a four-month pilot, Apollo Research can flag access gaps and explain how redactions affect what the public can verify.

OpenAI is bringing in Apollo Research, an outside AI safety group, for a four-month investigation into whether OpenAI’s models try to bypass safeguards. Apollo can disclose any limits OpenAI places on its access to evidence. OpenAI can redact confidential supporting evidence from the public reports, and Apollo can explain how any redactions affect its conclusions. The agreement protects Apollo’s verdict on each claim from redaction.

Six Apollo evaluators will work inside OpenAI. They will examine whether training encourages models to undermine safeguards, whether safety tests can draw out that behavior, whether it happens during internal use, and whether evaluators can detect the reasoning behind it.

OpenAI confirmed the engagement on November 16 as part of a first phase involving four outside assessors. OpenAI says Apollo is paid for its work and that compensation does not depend on the assessment’s results.

For the training inquiry, Apollo says it will have access to relevant code and datasets and be able to query intermediate training checkpoints. It will also get relevant reinforcement-learning records, including rewards and available reasoning traces. Access details for the other three inquiries were still to be agreed when Apollo announced the pilot on November 16.

The review covers agreed claims, models and time periods. OpenAI can withhold even in-scope material when it considers that reasonably necessary because of technical limits, security risks or intellectual property protection. It must tell Apollo that material was withheld and give written reasons. Where OpenAI considers it feasible and relevant, it will also identify the categories and approximate volume of withheld records. Unfavorable information alone cannot justify withholding access.

Apollo plans two public reports. The first will address whether training encouraged models to undermine safeguards. The second will cover safety testing, internal use and whether the reasoning behind such behavior can be detected. OpenAI can review the reports for confidential material, and its commitments bar it from requesting a redaction solely because a finding is unfavorable. The Safety and Security Committee of the OpenAI Foundation’s board will receive unredacted copies.

‘I Love Rock ‘n Roll’ Turns 45: An Oral History From Joan Jett, Kenny Laguna & More

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

"When it hit No. 1, the first thing I wanted to see was Billboard. I wanted to look at those charts," Jett shares of the iconic hit.

Joan Jett loved rock ‘n’ roll from the time she was an adolescent and swore to play it — especially after her first guitar teacher told her that “girls don’t play rock ‘n’ roll” and taught her “On Top of Old Smokey” instead. Explore Joan Jett Joan Jett & the Blackhearts See latest videos, charts and news

She certainly made good on that ambition.

Born Joan Larkin in suburban Philadelphia, she started playing guitar in Rockville, Md., and really pursued her passion after her family moved to Los Angeles County. She began haunting Rodney Bingenheimer’s English Disco and co-founded The Runaways with drummer Sandy West and producer/impresario Kim Fowley in 1975. Related Noah Kahan Reacts to 2027 Grammy Nominations Snub: 'I Was In My Feelings for a Sec' 'Grand Theft Auto VI' Revs Its Engines & Leads 2026 Game Awards Nominations: Full List Cody Johnson on Why He's Skipping 2026 CMA Awards: 'Going 100 MPH All the Time Isn't Healthy'

“She was a Suzi Quatro fan,” remembers latter-day Runaways and Jett manager Toby Mamis, now of Alive Enterprises, who met Jett when he was working with Quatro. “She planted herself in the hotel lobby, too shy to introduce herself or ask for an autograph. But we looked at her, this girl that dressed like Suzi … and I would encourage her. I thought that a great outlet for her devotion to Suzi was playing rock ‘n’ roll the way Suzi did, being inspired by Elvis.”

The Runaways lasted four studio albums, charting a couple of singles (“Cherry Bomb,” “Heartbeat”) on the Bubbling Under Hot 100 chart and making more noise in Japan than anywhere else. (The sordid story, including Foley’s abuse, is chronicled in the 2004 documentary Edgeplay: A Film About the Runaways and in the 2010 biopic The Runaways.)

The band’s dissolution during the spring 1979 left Jett moribund for a time, but after dealing with some health issues, she launched a solo career. Mamis recruited pop writer-producers Kenny Laguna and Ritchie Cordell, who first worked with Jett on We’re All Crazee Now! (a never-finished film based on the Runaways), and then on her first album in 1980, known as Joan Jett in Europe and Bad Reputation the following year, after Jett signed with Neil Bogart’s Boardwalk Records in the U.S.

Produced by Laguna and Cordell, with two tracks by Sex Pistols’ Steve Jones and Paul Cook, the album was an auspicious start for Jett — who herself had produced albums for several bands, including the Germs, around that time. Combining originals and covers, Bad Reputation reached No. 51 on the Billboard 200, while the title track and a rendition of Gary Glitter’s “Do You Wanna Touch Me (Oh Yeah)” and Lesley Gore’s “You Don’t Own Me” received a modicum of radio attention.

But the big breakthrough was on the near horizon.

I Love Rock ‘n Roll was recorded during the summer of 1981 and released on Nov. 18. Featuring Jett’s band the Blackhearts — a leather-clad, meat-and-potatoes rock quartet formed as she toured to support Bad Reputation — it was a defining 10-song collection of strong hooks, muscular arrangements and rocket-fueled energy, part craft, part chin-forward attitude. The troupe pulled out other covers, including the Dave Clark Five’s “Bits and Pieces,” the Halos’ “Nag” and the Runaways’ “You’re Too Possessive,” while Jett turned out four originals, including “Be Straight,” co-written with Laguna and Greg Kihn.

The album got off to a sluggish start; the first release included the Christmas staple “Little Drummer Boy,” which didn’t give the album much in the way of shelf life. After the holiday season, it was replaced with a Jett original, “Oh Woe Is Me,” and the title track — a 1975 song by the Arrows that Jett first heard while touring the U.K. with the Runaways — provided liftoff. The thumping alchemy of glam and punk hit No. 1 on the Billboard Hot 100 for seven weeks and Mainstream Rock Airplay for five weeks in 1982, followed by her cover of Tommy James and the Shondells’ “Crimson and Clover,” which went top 10 on the Hot 100. The I Love Rock ‘n Roll album peaked at No. 2 on the Billboard 200 and went platinum — enough to cement Jett’s place as a true queen of rock ‘n’ roll noise, whose reign continues 45 years and a 2015 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame induction later.

Jett, now 68, has seemed ageless over the years, clad in black and continuing to sling her guitar on stages around the world. “She’s such a force,” says Jane Wiedlin of the Go-Go’s, which followed the path Jett and the Runaways forged. “You can tell music is in her blood, and she’s always playing. She’s just… amazing. That’s all I can say.”

I Love Rock ‘n Roll’s sapphire anniversary, meanwhile, is being feted with a Record Store Black Friday release of I Love Rock ‘n Roll: The Shadow Album, a long-lost version of the set that was recorded during two-hour 1980 session at ODO Studios in New York. Though surprisingly finished-sounding, Jett and the Blackhearts — with Ricky Byrd replacing original guitarist Eric Ambel — opted to re-record the album for its ultimate release; the original recording remained lost, albeit in plain sight, at Laguna’s house until its recent discovery. On Dec. 11, a 45th anniversary deluxe edition will package both the original and The Shadow Album together.

To commemorate 45 years of putting another dime in the jukebox, baby, we’ve gathered some of the principals to dance with what they created back then.

Mamis managed the Runaways during the last year or so of their career, through their final album, And Now… The Runaways. He had approached Laguna but found him initially reticent. Jett, meanwhile, recorded three songs with the Sex Pistols’ Jones and Cook, including an early version of “I Love Rock ‘n Roll.” Ultimately, Mamis convinced him to help make the soundtrack for the We’re All Crazee Now! film.

Jett: When the Runaways broke up, I was pretty devastated; it was my band and I loved it. Once it broke up, I could feel the whole city of Los Angeles laughing: “We told you girls can’t play guitar! We told you they can’t rock!” It was a very depressing time. I was … not even partying. I was numbing myself. I thought about joining the military, ’cause what am I gonna do? I don’t have a skill. It was a very low period in my life.

Mamis: Without her band she was a bit rudderless. She had some health issues; there’s a picture of her playing guitar in a hospital room, playing guitar with these really cool headphones I got her that had built-in amplifiers so she could play loud and nobody else would hear it. I said, “When you get out of here we’re gonna launch your solo career. We’re gonna go to England and we’re gonna make a record.”

Jett: We had signed on to write a movie project right before the Runaways broke up. I still had to do this thing; I’d signed a contract and didn’t want to get sued. Toby knew Kenny, knew Kenny was a great producer and could write songs, so he put me together with Kenny to write songs for this movie.

Mamis: Kenny had previously turned me down on producing the last Runaways album; he wouldn’t even come see them in England when he was there recording something. Despite that, I just knew he was the right guy. So I called about the film soundtrack thing, and he said, “Could I bring Ritchie Cordell?” Well, absolutely, yeah. So they came out to L.A. and sat down at the Hyatt Hotel on Sunset the first day and came up with a gem of a song they were excited about … so we commenced working with them. The film never came out, but Kenny was determined to continue working with (Jett).

Jett: Kenny’s wife Meryl convinced him to come out and write with me; she said, “I think she’s significant.” So he did and we hit it off immediately. It was one of those things where you connected with somebody. The energy was good. I didn’t feel threatened. So we wrote songs for this movie, and then Kenny wanted to help me realize my dreams. I told him I wanted it to be guys; I didn’t need the endless Runaways comparisons that would happen.

Laguna: I had five songs on a tape: “Bad Reputation,” “I Love Rock ‘n Roll,” “Crimson and Clover,” “Do You Wanna Touch Me” and “You Don’t Own Me.” All hits, I thought. That tape went to 23 labels, and everybody turned it down.

Jett: We still have all the letters; “You need a song search,” “Tell her to lose the guitar,” all insulting stuff. That tells you they don’t listen to the music people send them, ’cause they missed out on all these songs that made a significant dent on the charts.

Laguna: Clive Davis wrote us back that, “Joan’s an interesting artist, but she’d need a song search.” I wrote back, “If you signed Cole Porter, you’d probably do a song search!” So he didn’t sign four, five hits. We had a thing with Ariola, which became our label in England and we were going to just put it out ourselves (in the U.S.) until Boardwalk picked it up and changed the name, because Neal Bogart was a genius.

The first album featured a cadre of players, including a “house band” dubbed the Roll-Ups that included Laguna on keyboards and backing vocals. Others contributing included Clem Burke and Frank Infante of Blondie, who Mamis was also working with, as well as future Blackheart Ambel. In its wake, the Jett camp began filling out the band, holding auditions at S.I.R. Rehearsal Studios in Los Angeles after running an ad in music magazines reading “Joan Jett is looking for a few good men. Showoffs need not apply.”

Mamis: We had auditions for several days in a row, and John Doe, a friend from the L.A. scene and a bass player, came down and helped us evaluate drummers. We brought a few back the next day to play with a variety of bass players, and John sat down and evaluated the bass players and we narrowed that field down and the guitar players… we put the Blackhearts together. Joan came up with the name and I booked some shows for them in Europe to tie in to the album release and then we booked some shows in L.A. — clubs, residencies — for four weeks in a row, two shows a night, all to break the band in. Chrissie Hynde showed up at the Whisky one night ’cause she came from Ohio as a Suzi Quatro and Runaways fan and wanted to see the show and meet Joan.

Jett: I wanted to form a band, but I wanted it to be guys. I didn’t want endless Runaways comparisons that would happen if I did it with (women) again. So we formed a version of the Blackhearts in Los Angeles and we moved everybody back East… ’cause there’s a more robust touring scene. You can hit five states pretty easily and cover more ground than you could back in L.A. So we were writing songs and getting our set together, and we started to gain fans … and were selling the (first) record out of the trunk of our car at gigs.

The I Love Rock ‘n Roll Shadow Album was recorded with original Blackhearts — Ambel and Gary Ryan on bass, with recent recruit Lee Crystal on drums. The addition of New York native Byrd from the band Susan, meanwhile, was a crucial move that brought a tougher element into the mix. He’d stay with Jett for 10 years, recording six more albums with her.

Byrd: Carol Kaye, my wife, was a publicist at Leber Krebs, this giant company that managed Aerosmith, Ted Nugent, Humble Pie. Joan and Kenny were friends with Steve Leber, and they didn’t have an office; they were selling the Bad Reputation record basically out of the car. So Steve Leber gave them a closet in the back of Leber Krebs, and Carol became friendly with Joan. I saw the Runaways at CBGB; I remember standing on the side of the stage. The band Susan had done our thing and I was trying to figure out what was next. Then Carol said, “Would you be interested in going down and jamming with (Jett)?” I said, “Of course.” It was as simple as that.

Mamis: Ricky had a different kind of approach to the guitar than Eric did. Eric played great guitar, but he’s not as assertive of rock player. Ricky was more of an AOR, radio-friendly rock guitarist, more of a lead guitar player. Joan was de-emphasizing showoffs and lead guitar, but I think that Kenny, as a producer, saw that the recordings needed more lead guitar and lead guitar attracted fans. That’s the difference from the Bad Reputation album; the I Love Rock ‘n Roll album is more guitar-heavy in terms of the leads.

Byrd: There was no pressure. I joined a band, and we started doing gigs, so we got to know each other and I weaved in and out of her rhythms. Ritchie and Kenny knew what the guitar sound was they wanted, and I just played the way I play. That black (Gibson) Les Paul I used on the whole record is in the Rock & Roll Hall of Fame now.

Jett: We were just feeling our way; the band had to fit around me. I don’t think I was specifically after (a sound). I didn’t hear something that I wanted to make. I just wanted to make those sounds I heard in my head when I started to play guitar — Marc Bolan and T. Rex, (David) Bowie, a dirty, strong rhythm guitar (like) Keith Richards. I wasn’t sure, but I knew it when I had it and I would know if I didn’t like it. There wasn’t a lot of experimentation; everyone did what they did, and it came together naturally.

The glammy “I Love Rock ‘n Roll Song” was written by Alan Merrill and Jake Hooker of the British band Arrows, released as a single in 1975 as, Merrill told Songfacts, “a knee-jerk response” to the Rolling Stones’ “It’s Only Rock ‘n Roll (But I Like It).” Produced by Mickie Most, it started out as a B-side but was eventually flipped to the A-side and the group performed the song on its self-titled ITV television series the following year.

Jett: I was in England and watching this TV show and this band called the Arrows was on and they were playing the B-side of whatever their single was at the time. The B-side was “I Love Rock ‘n Roll,” and I thought, “Wow, I like that song. It sounds like a hit.” It just really grabbed me. So I went to a record store and bought the 45 and said, “I want the Runaways to do this, maybe.” But we had done a (Velvet Underground) song, “Rock & Roll,” on the first album and everyone was like, “We don’t want to do another song with rock and roll in the title,” which I understood.

Laguna: Joan told the Runaways it could be a hit, and they didn’t think so. They were thinking very small — no vision, in my opinion.

Jett: So I thought, “I’m gonna hang onto this,” ’cause you never know. I knew the Runaways wouldn’t last forever. And after the Runaways broke up, I knew I wanted to try the song.

Mamis: One day, after (the Runaways) made their first album and were getting to work on their second, (Jett) was at my apartment in West Hollywood. I played her some records I thought would be pretty cool for the Runaways to cover, and I played “I Love Rock ‘n Roll’ by Arrows. She looked at me: “I know this song! It’s great!” So I made a cassette and Joan brought it to Kim. He was interested, but when Mickie Most wouldn’t give him a share of the American publishing rights, he refused to record it with the Runaways. So the Runaways didn’t cover it at the time, but Joan kept it in her pocket. When we started working on her solo career, I said, “We still have to do this song” and she said, “We absolutely have to do this song.” She knew it was a great song.

Jett: I think Toby had connections with the Sex Pistols management in England. It may have been a matter of the Sex Pistols had broken up, and I went in and did the song with Steve and Paul in England (during March 1979).

Mamis: Kenny felt the version we did with Steve Jones and Paul Cook wasn’t strong enough, wasn’t mainstream enough. He knew it was a good song, worth re-recording.

Laguna: When we did Bad Reputation I said, “This is very commercial, this song, but we should wait until we have our ducks in a row. We’re just too small now. Let’s save it ’til the next record.”

Byrd: The first time I heard (“I Love Rock ‘n Roll”) was when I joined the band. It was a very simplistic song. I remember going to Leslie West, who was a friend of mine and a huge influence on my guitar playing when I was a kid, and asked him, “What kind of solo should I play on this?” He goes, “Just play the melody,” so that’s what I tried to do. I think it’s a pretty good simple guitar solo that’s still played by everybody, all the cover bands that do “I Love Rock ‘n Roll.”

Jane Wiedlin: I knew the song before, from the (Arrows) version of it. Her version is so amazing; she has the whole glam rock element to her sound, which I love because I was a glam rocker before I became a punk rocker. It’s a powerful song. It never gets old, and everyone loves it. There’s nothing not to love about it.

Byrd: Did we know it was gonna be a huge song? I don’t know if anybody knows anything; you put stuff out there and it either hits a nerve or it doesn’t. I’ll tell you one thing: all these many years later you can walk into any Stop & Shop or any store anywhere and it’ll come on the sound system to this day. And if I’m with my kid she just looks at me and rolls her eyes.

The session captured on The Shadow Album was recorded after weeks of rehearsing and gigging before settling in at ODO Studios in New York. That version of the Blackhearts — Ambel, Ryan and Crystal — was in fighting form and, playing live, laid down initial recordings of the 10 album tracks.

Laguna: Electric Lady offered us a few hours to do an album, ’cause we had a great buzz on the street and they wanted us to try their studio. They gave us two bottles of champagne, and while we were laying down tracks and not watching Joan, being one of a crazy scene of punk rockers, she drank the two bottles. When it came time to sing, she couldn’t sing and the session was scrapped.

Laguna: We did the 10 or 12 songs and got them down and really tight. We went into it like a demo; Joan remembers two hours, I remember one hour, but we went in and recorded with Joan singing in an (isolation) booth and her amp outside the booth and that live vocal. When we listened to it we said, “Boy, this is really good. Maybe this is the album.”

Jett: We just ran it like a set, just recorded everything and it sounded really awesome. It’s just fun and raw. We had one more song we didn’t do in that session, “Run Away,” so we went to another studio (Kingdom Sound on Long Island) and played the song and it was head and shoulders above what we had just recorded with The Shadow Album. So we decided to do the whole thing again; we knew we could get everything sounding a little better but still be raw, the way we wanted it to sound.

Laguna: We had a better engineer (Glen Kolotkin) and a better studio … It was just superior. So I called Ritchie and said, “I think we’re not looking at the truth; we both know we should record the album over.” “Oh no, we can’t do that! That’s insane!” So we had six days left and we recorded the whole album, and that was the I Love Rock ‘n Roll album.

Jett: I’m glad we found (The Shadow Album) because it really gives a view into what we sounded like in those really early days. We just ran it like a set, just recorded everything and it sounded really awesome. It’s just fun and raw. When I listen to it I find it really interesting, the sort of rhythmic ways that I sang the songs and phrase things … but it’s very similar to what we are now.

Laguna: It’s a miracle we found this (tape). We own this house together on Long Island and Joan used to live with us there. There’s a guest room, and on the shelves was the (original) “Crimson and Clover” tape and I think they found The Shadow Album in the basement of my house. We said, “It can’t be this simple that I was sleeping 10 feet from it for 25 years!” It’s such a miracle.

Richie Cordell had been a principal songwriter, along with Bo Gentry, for Tommy James and the Shondells, penning hits such as “I Think We’re Alone Now” and “Mony Mony.” “Crimson and Clover,” however, marked a sea change, with James getting complete creative control from Roulette Records and writing on his own — albeit still with Gentry for this. James’ version was released in 1968 and hit No. 1 on the Hot 100 for two weeks in 1969. Jett’s version hit No. 7 in 1982, and she caused a bit of a controversy by not changing the pronouns from James’ version, which referenced romantic intentions toward a woman.

Jett: Kenny played in Tommy James’ band on a lot of the hits. I heard Tommy’s version and said, “I’d really like to try and do that (song), and just keep it the same.”

Laguna: You found it in my record collection, and we talked about it.

Jett: I asked you what it was about. You said Tommy said it was about, like, an acid trip, it wasn’t necessarily about a girl.

Laguna: I didn’t know what Tommy meant by the song. It wasn’t a love song, per se, as much as it was, like, an acid trip. It was just a psychedelic song and a gorgeous melody; it doesn’t really mean anything except what you want it to mean. Tommy had a vision in his head that those words would go together.

Jett: It’s just a song I was a fan of and I thought it would be good to do, and people loved it.

Laguna: Joan just did it fooling around at a sound check, and the people cleaning up the place and getting ready for the gigs put down the mops and started clapping — maybe only 30 people there, but that was significant. They were reacting to what she just did. So we saw that happen and we put it in the set and people loved it. It got a much bigger reaction than “I Love Rock ‘n Roll.”

Jett: I didn’t change the (gender) in the lyrics because … why? It would be stupid to change it to a “him.” I related to it, if you understand what I mean. What are you afraid of? I get a lot of women and girls telling me how important that song was, just because I didn’t change the words. For some reason, it meant a lot to a lot of people that I had the courage to do it, but I really didn’t think about it. It’s an instinct thing. If I feel something’s right, I just do it and deal with the consequences later.

Laguna: Both Joan and I said, “You’ve got to keep it the way it was — ‘I hardly know her but I think I could love her.’” It felt right to us that Joan, a woman artist, would sing the pronouns feminine. Some of the people who heard that thought it was not doable, but in the end it turned out to be a really cool thing. The kids liked it. Kathleen Hanna said in the documentary Bad Reputation that as a kid listening to it, it blew her mind; who was this girl who didn’t change the pronouns? For Kathleen, that was a major thing and Joan was not afraid of keeping the gender the way she thought the song should be.

Byrd: They had already cut that with Eric, so I just kind of jumped on what Eric did and added my two cents. It’s pretty similar to Tommy James’; it just rocks a little harder.

Among the more intriguing covers on the album was “You’re Too Possessive,” a Jett composition that closed the Runaways’ third album, Waitin’ For the Night, which was the group’s first as a quartet after the departure of singer Cherie Currie.

Laguna: It was part of a conscious thing we did. We thought we could reintroduce those songs by the Runaways that were way underrated — as good as any of the guy groups. If (Jett) had been a boy, it would be perceived differently.

Jett: The lyrics still totally speak to what goes on either in my life or other people’s lives, where they can attach to situations they’re in. I try to write as many songs as I can to reflect on my life or people who I know’s lives, things I can actually speak to.

Laguna: Our concept for the Blackhearts being the solo comeback for Joan was we were going to try to do a Runaways song on every album, with the Blackhearts, ’cause they had been overlooked by the industry and radio. But these songs she was writing, at 16 years old, were just fantastic … and we wanted to get more respect for them.

Laguna: (Bogart) thought the hit was “Little Drummer Boy.” I was old school; you can’t have a hit anymore at Christmastime because it takes three weeks to make it a hit, and so you’ve got to put it out in November. By the end of three weeks (the industry) took a break at the end of the year; the last two weeks of the year there was no radio, there was no adding things. The whole industry closes down. You have two weeks to make a hit. So we argued about that; I wanted “Crimson and Clover” and “Victim of Circumstance,” and Neal wanted “Little Drummer Boy.” Then, all of a sudden, “I Love Rock ‘n Roll” started crawling up in January, crawling up in February and went on radio as the No. 1 most requested.

Boardwalk finally released “I Love Rock ‘n Roll” as a single on Jan. 20, 1982, along with a black-and-white music video featuring Jett and the Blackhearts playing in a dive bar (the Privates in Manhattan’s Upper East Side) that was embraced by a fledgling MTV. The single, meanwhile, quickly swelled on radio playlists.

Laguna: We saw the writing on the wall. I was in Florida, driving on (Interstate) 95 and “I Love Rock ‘n Roll” was on the radio. I pressed the button after it was over and there’s “I Love Rock ‘n Roll” again. And then it happened a third time, and when it finished, the DJ said, “There you have it; our No. 1 record this week.” That wasn’t for the country, just for Florida, but I was so overcome with emotion I couldn’t drive. I had to pull over on the side of the shoulder to get my breath, ’cause I couldn’t believe it. That was the first time the universe was saying, “Something is happening for you guys.”

Jett: I’ll never forget; when it hit No. 1, the first thing I wanted to see was Billboard. I wanted to look at those charts and see the song up there. I get chills thinking about it even now …’cause it was so unlikely in the scheme of things, the way everything was stacked against us. We were just this little band, struggling to make it and then….

Byrd: Did we know it was gonna be a huge song? I don’t know if anybody knows anything; you put stuff out there and it either hits a nerve or it doesn’t. When you’re in the middle of it, when you’re young and you’re riding through it, you don’t realize what’s going on. We do the record, it comes out, we hit the road and we’re playing clubs. Everybody’s piled into this Winnebago, crew and band and management. We don’t know what the hell is going on. What we do see is the venues start getting more crowded and the venues start getting bigger and now we’re starting to open for these giant bands. Then all of a sudden we’re getting a tour bus. Once a week or something, Kenny Laguna or Meryl would come on the bus, “Look at Billboard!” and you see it climbing up the charts. But we were in the middle of it. We’re just a rock ‘n’ roll band, bro. We just want to go out there and get sweaty.

Besides establishing Jett’s enduring career, “I Love Rock ‘n Roll” has enjoyed a robust afterlife. “Weird Al” Yankovic and LadBaby both parodied it as “I Love Rocky Road” and “I Love Sausage Rolls,” respectively — the latter a Christmas No. 1 in the U.K. in 2019. Britney Spears had a worldwide hit with her version from the 2002 film Crossroads. In 2016, Jett’s recording was inducted into the Grammy Hall of Fame.

Jett: It was definitely a vindication. “I’m not going away” was the message, “I love this and the audience seems to love it and I’m gonna keep it up.” It was very satisfying to hear your songs on the radio and turn it all the way up and roll your windows down in the car. That was the fantasy you had when you were a kid about being in a band and making it — it wasn’t as much about being famous, it was more to be in communion with other people and be in a space and look them in the eye and share that connection.

Mamis: I felt really vindicated when she broke through. Really vindicated. Joan is a great artist. I never lost faith in her, even when I stopped working with her. I always wanted her to succeed, so when she did it really justified my belief in her and the contributions I made. And I still think she’s great.

Laguna: In the meantime, I still didn’t believe the Bad Reputation record was done. I always thought “Do You Wanna Touch Me” could be a hit. It just showed like a hit — when we played it live, and people were requesting on the radio. [Re-released as a single in 1982, it peaked at No. 20 on the Hot 100.]

Jett: I also felt the whole industry saying, “OK, you did it yourself. Do it again,” in a way. I might be reading into it a degree, but I felt people felt dissed or something ’cause we were able to do it ourselves. So we did it (again). Sometimes it feels like 45 minutes, sometimes it feels like 45 years. I’ve been really lucky in my life, and blessed that I’ve been able to do what I wanted to do and have so many things happen in my life.

GTA 6's frame rate isn't flawless, but it's substantially better than how its predecessors launched

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

GTA 6 is finally here – well, depending on your time zone, anyway. In any case, it's a real video game that people are actually playing, which means it's time to discuss the game's most important aspect: its resolution and frame rate. According to a new tech analysis, Rockstar has managed to coax solid performance out of the current-gen consoles even while pursuing nigh-unprecedented levels of fidelity.

"GTA 6 is a 30 frames per second game, but that also kind of makes it the smoothest GTA game in history, at least at launch," as tech analyst John Linneman explains in a new Digital Foundry video. "Every previous entry ran significantly worse on the original target hardware. Whether that trade-off is worthwhile, however, is entirely up to the individual. But as things stand, I'm not convinced that they could have achieved 60 frames per second in a game of this scope on this class of hardware."

It's now trivial to play previous GTA games at 60fps or above, but each previous 3D entry targeted 30fps on its original platforms and regularly dipped below that target. GTA 6's hold on its 30fps target is much stronger.

On base PS5 and Xbox Series X (which Digital Foundry covered in a separate analysis), the game will dip below 30fps at times when, say, screaming down the road at speed, but those dips are "very minor." If you really try to break the game by heading into a busy intersection with a grenade launcher and causing chaos, you can cause the frame rate to take a more sustained dive, but you'll get a solid experience in the course of normal play.

While you can get some dips with the kind of chaos on PS5 Pro, the whole experience seems to be even smoother on Sony's high-end hardware. "I spent most of my time on the PS5 Pro, which is the best way to play this game by far," Linneman says. "You basically get a near-flawless 30fps update. The frame pacing is perfect, cutscenes consistent and fluid, unlike older Rockstar titles, and everything moves without hiccups or stutters."

Yes, that does also mean Rockstar's finally fixed the cutscene judder that's plagued its games since GTA 4. The miracles continue.

Well, there is one exception, as you probably shouldn't expect any miracles on Xbox Series S. Microsoft's lower-end machine retains most of the graphical features of its more fully-featured counterparts, which I suppose is miraculous in its own way, but its frame rate brings us back to the peak of Xbox 360 gaming, with sustained drops into the 20fps range whenever there's real action on-screen.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

While Rockstar's decision to go 30fps with GTA 6 has been controversial, Linneman is "more willing to forgive it due to just how far it's pushing the visuals." He adds, "it feels like tasting the future."

Check out our full GTA 6 review if you're interested in, you know, the actual game rather than the frame rates.

His love for games was sparked somewhere between Metal Gear Solid 2 and Knights of the Old Republic, and these days you can usually find him splitting his entertainment time between retro gaming, the latest big action-adventure title, or a long haul in American Truck Simulator.

I'm modding GTA 5 every day in a quest to either transform it into a PC version of GTA 6 or break it entirely. Come back tomorrow to find out what new horrible thing I've done to Rockstar's baby, or check out what I got up to earlier.

Well, it's almost time. The big day is upon us: Grand Theft Auto 6 is here.

I mean, not here. We don't have it. But over in consoleland my understanding is the masses are—as we speak—building some sort of life-partnership with their new husband/wife. Also, doing crimes. Also, shooting kneecaps.

Home Depot’s incoming CEO says homeowners are ‘feeling pressure.’ A quarter can’t cover a $5,000 repair

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Richard McPhail, a 21-year Home Depot veteran who was named the retailer’s next CEO on Tuesday, has been watching financial pressure build among American homeowners. On the company’s third-quarter earnings call the same day, he acknowledged that “a rising interest rate environment puts pressure on our customer and our business.”

McPhail, Home Depot’s CFO since 2019, takes over as CEO on Nov. 23. Ted Decker, who recently returned from a medical leave, will step down as CEO and remain chair of the board.

That’s a problem for a retailer that booked $164.7 billion in revenue last fiscal year, the bulk of it from people fixing up homes they already own. Homeowners are “one of the most resilient customer cohorts in the economy,” McPhail told Yahoo Finance in a February interview. “But they’re feeling pressure like everyone else.”

“If you look at housing turnover, that’s actually been frozen for three years,” he said, adding that turnover has sat at historical lows three years running. He also pointed to customers’ “concerns around housing affordability, general affordability” and “broader concerns over uncertainty and job loss.”

Owning a home has long been considered a path to financial security in America. But even homeowners with considerable housing wealth feel squeezed by the rising cost of living, and some struggle to afford the upkeep of their biggest asset.

Nearly two-thirds of homeowners (63%) say owning a home causes them at least moderate financial stress, up from 55% in June 2025, according to an October survey of 1,000 homeowners conducted for Hometap, a home equity financing company.

That stress comes even as Americans sit on a record $13.2 trillion in accessible home equity as of the second quarter of 2026, according to First American Data & Analytics. That’s the amount owners could borrow against while keeping their combined loan-to-value at 80% or below.

But housing wealth isn’t disposable income. Even owners who locked in rock-bottom pandemic-era mortgage rates aren’t immune to rising property taxes and insurance premiums or climbing utility bills. The typical homeowner now spends $16,566 a year on costs beyond the mortgage, about two-thirds of it on upkeep, according to Zillow.

Job worries are adding to the strain. In the Federal Reserve Bank of New York’s October Survey of Consumer Expectations, released last week, consumers put the odds of higher unemployment a year from now at 45.3%, the highest reading since April 2020. Their perceived chance of losing their own job also ticked up to 13.9%.

A LendingTree survey conducted in September found that 34% of homeowners had postponed at least one necessary repair or maintenance task over the past two years. Among those with outstanding repairs, 32% said they simply couldn’t afford the work.

A surprise $5,000 repair would be entirely out of reach for a quarter of homeowners surveyed. Another 30% could cover it only by substantially draining their savings.

Among homeowners who needed repairs over the past two years, 37% dipped into emergency savings, and 33% cut spending on essentials such as groceries, healthcare, or childcare to pay for the work.

Waiting tends to cost more. Among respondents whose homes currently need repairs, 34% said delaying the work drove up the price, and a quarter said it caused damage elsewhere in the house.

Decker sounded a similar alarm in February, telling analysts that “our customers are telling us that they’re not investing” in bigger projects, citing affordability, the jobs picture, and weak consumer confidence.

Even so, the business has grown this year. Home Depot reported third-quarter sales of $43.4 billion on Tuesday, up 4.9% from a year earlier, with comparable sales up 2.2%. “Third quarter results exceeded our expectations,” McPhail said.

Homeowners may be shelving kitchen remodels, but smaller repairs and maintenance jobs have kept customers coming in. In August, McPhail, then CFO, credited “broad-based demand across the business as customers continued to engage in smaller projects.”

Tight budgets are also pushing homeowners to pick up the tools themselves. Among LendingTree respondents who completed at least some repairs, 60% did some of the work themselves because they couldn’t afford a professional. Among millennials, that figure reached 72%.

At the other end of the market, Home Depot has been courting professional contractors, builders, and remodelers. SRS, the building-materials distributor it acquired for $18.25 billion in June 2024, delivered “almost $5 billion in sales in the quarter,” McPhail said. Executives said on the call that cross-selling between Home Depot and SRS is on track to generate $400 million in sales this year, a figure the company aims to double in 2027.

The company has also been investing in AI and digital tools that help pros plan projects, manage material lists, and track orders. Its express delivery service now reaches customers within two hours from virtually all of its stores, incoming president and COO Ann-Marie Campbell said on the call.

Home Depot now expects total sales to grow about 4.5% in fiscal 2026, with comparable sales up roughly 1.4%. McPhail is betting that speed will keep winning over a cautious customer: “Our investments in express delivery and in AI customer and associate enablement are making a meaningful difference.”

How Thinking Outcomes Can Be More Important Than Being Right

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 18, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

A few months back, I wrote a piece here about the viral conspiracy that Earth would lose gravity for 7 seconds on August 12th. I discussed how, despite not actually believing that the event would occur—because of a slew of reasons—the open-minded part of me (albeit still skeptical) nevertheless suggested developing a quick “just in case” protocol for the unlikely event. I received some positive feedback on how I exemplified the importance of cautious thinking throughout the piece and how, in context, we can engage two important dispositions towards thinking (Dwyer et al., 2016): open-mindedness and skepticism. On the other hand, though, some implied a level of foolishness in my thinking.

The latter cohort, I believe, missed my point, and so I aim to make it clearer in this follow-up. In that previous piece, I stated on multiple occasions that I didn’t actually believe the event would occur. In fact, I highlighted why it was nearly impossible.

But I used this event prediction as an example to make the point that cautious thinkers are typically happy to make minor mistakes in efforts to avoid risk, even if the risk is extremely low. That is, through cost-benefit analysis (consistent with many existing cost-benefit models we commonly see in psychology; e.g. Lave and March 1975; Thibaut and Kelley 1959; Uehara 1990), good thinkers may often choose a 99 percent likely mistake made out of caution, if the mistake yields minimal negative impact (e.g., looking foolish in front of one’s spouse). This will be selected over doing nothing potentially “foolish” (e.g., in light of being 99 percent “sure”), despite the disastrous impact that 1 percent chance would have because they failed to act (e.g., if the event occurred, death would be a near certainty if we were outdoors).

Simply, good thinkers don’t care about labels of wrong or right; they care about the outcomes of their decisions. It’s cautious thinking… and critical thinkers are cautious thinkers.

It’s much like hope for the best, plan for the worst. It’s why we develop contingency plans; it’s why we buy insurance. It’s largely why I don’t go outside in a thunderstorm, despite the odds against getting hit by lightning. It’s why I buy a lotto ticket once every few weeks, when the thought occurs to me on the day of a draw (i.e., I will not notice the loss of a couple of euros [thankfully], whereas winning the lotto would be life-changing). You never know.

With that, context (as well as moderation) is key. It might be foolish to buy too much insurance, to spend €200 weekly on the lotto, or to waste your time hiding in a cellar for three hours during your run-of-the-mill thunderstorm. Again, caution is considered in terms of cost (be it time, money, or status) relative to benefit or risk avoidance.

From an epistemological perspective, an established truth is that we cannot see into the future (as of yet, anyway [there’s some doozy of a meta-joke in there]). We do not know what’s going to happen. Objectively, we can place likelihoods on certain events happening, but that’s it. It’s our human interpretation that labels these probabilities as impossible, unlikely, possible, likely, highly probable, definite, etc. We might say definite, but that’s not exactly a guarantee. Consider the late Daniel Kahneman’s work on weather and stock forecasting (see also Kahneman, 2011). If we lived in a world of certainty (which we don’t), you’d never hear the phrase, “Well, that was unexpected.”

In moments of excitement and uncertainty, good thinkers are able to quickly evaluate probabilities, not just in terms of odds, but in terms of interpretation of outcomes (e.g., consider naturalistic decision-making [Klein, 2008]). The ability to do this quickly is a result of previous consideration and reflective judgment on priorities and values—things for which good thinkers would be concerned and cautious. They apply these considerations efficiently, if and when they need to. For example, despite less than a half-percent chance of your school burning down during your tenure in that school, how many fire drills were you a part of as a student? A lot, right? But you knew what to do in case it was for real.

Bizarrely, what’s your plan for a zombie apocalypse? Stop for a moment and think about it... Did your answer come to you fairly quickly? Even in the extreme unlikelihood of such a thing happening, it’s likely you thought about it or something similar before, at least once.

There’s nothing wrong with considering such things. Planning in such a way is a good cognitive exercise. It’s also good for creativity!

Cautious people often think in similar ways. Such cautious thinking might get accused of being “overthinking.” But that’s not necessarily a bad thing, unless it becomes a common or costly focus. Indeed, catastrophizing can be problematic. Moderation is important. But so too is caution, especially when it comes to ensuring good outcome-focused thinking. So, don’t sweat making small mistakes in life, especially if it’s done out of calculated caution. There’s nothing foolish about it.

Dwyer, C. P., Hogan, M. J., Harney, O. M., & Kavanagh, C. (2017). Facilitating a student-educator conceptual model of dispositions towards critical thinking through interactive management. Educational Technology Research and Development, 65(1), 47-73.

Kahneman, D. (2011). Thinking fast and slow. Penguin: Great Britain.

Klein, Gary A. (2008). Naturalistic Decision Making. Human Factors: The Journal of the Human Factors and Ergonomics Society, 50 (3): 456–460.

Lave, C. A., & March, J. G. (1975). An introduction to models in the social sciences. New York: Harper & Row.

Thibaut, J. W., & Kelley, H. H. (1959). The social psychology of groups. New York: Wiley.

Uehara, E. (1990). Dual exchange theory, social networks, and informal social support. The American Journal of Sociology, 96, 521-557.

Trinidad Navarro knows how to hunt down wrongdoing.

Google’s AI feature in Search gets its own incognito mode

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google’s search engine has long offered an incognito mode for all those search queries you don’t want logged or associated with your account. Now, Google’s AI Mode in Search is getting a similar feature. In a blog post, the company announced a new feature that brings “temporary threads” to AI Mode, where the conversations you have won’t be resurfaced later in areas like your search history or recommendations.

Many leading AI chatbots already offer the option for temporary chats, Google Gemini included. However, there was not yet an option to have a temporary chat when you’re searching Google and are dropped into its conversational interface, known as AI Mode.

Google says that users won’t have to adjust their settings to take advantage of the new option. Instead, they’ll be able to click a temporary threads icon in the upper right corner of AI Mode to engage in a temporary chat. This essentially works the same as a temporary chat would in the stand-alone Gemini chatbot.

The option could be convenient to use at those times when you start digging deeper into a particular topic via AI Mode and realize that you don’t want the discussion to become a part of your search history because of its personal nature.

Once the temporary thread is started, the session becomes detached from your account, Google says. This means that the responses the AI provides won’t be informed by past preferences, nor will they impact future personalization. That could also be useful for those times when you want to learn about something as more of a one-off, but don’t want Google to start to think of the topic as one of your main interests.

When you close out of the thread, you will return to searching as usual.

The company says the option to engage in temporary chats in AI Mode is rolling out globally on desktop and mobile across all supported languages.

Glen Powell Tussles with Country Music Superstars in <i><em>The Comeback King </em></i>Trailer

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Powell plays a country singer on the rebound in the upcoming Judd Apatow comedy.

Rodney Dangerfield set the bar for return-to-school comedies in 1986’s aptly titled Back to School. He decried that “If a man has no education, he's got nothing” before proceeding to throw money at all of his problems and hilariously ignore his own advice for the next hour and a half. Then Adam Sandler put his own, unique spin on the film when he sent Billy Madison back to grade school a decade later. And when The Comeback King hits theaters on February 5, 2027, Glen Powell will walk into homeroom as a country singer looking to save his career by finishing his degree.

In the first trailer, which debuted on Wednesday, November 18, Powell plays Aubrey King, a country music superstar dating the biggest pop star in the world (played by Madelyn Cline). His life is turned upside down when she cheats on him and then writes a song blaming him for the deed instead, which sends his career down the toilet. So, his manager convinces him to head back to school and rediscover what he wants to say with his music. Plus, fall in love with a teacher (Cristin Milioti) and joke around with The Comeback King’s insane list of guest stars—including Miranda Lambert, Jelly Roll, Brad Paisley, Chris Stapleton, Shaboozey, and Reba McEntire.

Powell and director Judd Apatow mostly wrote The Comeback King as they filmed it—which is normal for Apatow, but not so much for Powell. “For a guy like me, who likes to be prepared, it’s a nightmare,” Powell told Esquire in our cover story last month. The script came together from the best lines of their improvisational takes. Spitfires of one joke after another until Apatow laughs, or says “That’s funny,” Powell says. But for now, there’s little of the actual schooling revealed in the trailer.

You can catch a lot of the jokes from our celebrity cameos, however, like when Jelly Roll tells a disheveled Powell that he “smells like how people think I smell.” There’s also a great line when one of Powell’s managers, played by Kumail Nanjiani, says that fans answered a poll about him and said that his eyes were too close together, which wasn’t even one of the options. “They wrote it in like you were a Libertarian candidate,” he says. Watch the trailer above.

The Comeback King begins a busy 2027 for Powell, who also has the sci-fi film The Great Beyond coming out in October by J.J. Abrams. Also in the works? A Ron Howard firefighter movie that is reportedly not connected to Backdraft; the third season of his Hulu football comedy series, Chad Powers; a Lou Gehrig film with Richard Linklater; and likely the start of Top Gun 3—whenever Tom Cruise is ready. As Powell joked to Esquire, “Hopefully I’m not the age of Maverick in Maverick by the time I make it.”
