It shouldn’t be this easy to pedal 25 mph: Specialized’s Turbo Vado 3 Evo

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Travel  |  Read Source →

This commuter bike will have you blazing through streets without any range anxiety.

I’ve had only one experience of pedaling an e-bike after it ran out of juice, but it was enough to make checking the battery display an anxiety-driven habit: “Can I afford to use the highest assist level up this hill? Should I switch to eco mode to preserve power and make sure I have enough to get home with the bike lights on?”

These aren’t fun questions to have rattling around in your head while riding home in the dark.

But those intrusive thoughts never crossed my mind while I was testing Specialized’s Turbo Vado 3 Evo. I didn’t experience range anxiety, even while blowing through city streets at 25-plus mph (40.2 kph). The bike has so much juice (840 watt-hours) and power (810 watts of peak power and 105 newton-meters of torque) from Specialized’s 3.1 motor system that I could ride as far and as fast as I wanted without worrying about having enough battery to get back home.

Given my preference for biking over driving, I need a bike that can get me across town at a decent clip without draining an entire charge. That’s exactly what the Evo delivered on my longest rides.

What sold me on the bike was a 24-mile round trip that took 83 minutes and used just 56 percent of the battery. According to Specialized’s app, which connects to the bike over Bluetooth, I averaged 235 W of assist power while moving an average of 17 mph, though I was generally closer to 20 mph on stretches where I didn’t have to slow down. I also hit a max speed of 27.3 mph with little effort.

The bike is a beast, weighing around 70 pounds for the large-size frame I tested. Despite being the heaviest e-bike I’ve ridden, the Evo makes pedaling easier than ever. The bike lacks a throttle, so it takes a few rotations to get moving, but once the assist kicks in, it’s remarkable how easily you can reach the 18–20 mph range.

That higher speed comes from the bike’s Sport mode, which became my default setting. It requires a bit more pedaling to reach full assist, which explains why my smartwatch recorded my heart rate in the moderate-to-vigorous zone if I was riding above 20 mph.) The other modes are Eco (minimal assist, with you supplying most of the power), Turbo (max power with little effort, making uphill climbs trivial), and Auto (which adjusts assist level to your pedaling). You can also tune each setting to your liking.

The added bulk is also meant to handle rougher terrain. I’ve had the occasional flat tire from road debris on slimmer bikes, so I appreciate the sturdier, all-terrain, flat-resistant tires. Still, the 70-pound frame makes me hesitant to venture too far from pavement. I won’t be bombing down any mountain bike trails, but it’s helpful to have the option to jump off a curb or ride on dirt or gravel roads.

The weight makes the bike markedly different from the previous Turbo Vado we reviewed; this isn’t a bike I’d want to ride without assistance. Even the Eco mode, which you’d have to use exclusively to get the bike’s purported 150-km (93-mile), five-hour range, felt a bit sluggish compared to the more powerful modes I preferred. Specialized also sells a 280-Whr range extender, but at $700, it feels like overkill.

The RockShox Psylo suspension fork, with 120 mm of travel, also made this the smoothest-riding e-bike I’ve tested. Other e-bikes I’ve ridden have felt stiff over even small bumps in the road, but the Evo’s bounciness made hitting bumps feel like a nice change of pace. I used to be scared of going over a curb, and that’s no longer the case. The 40 mm dropper seatpost is another nice feature, giving riders a bit of travel and letting them lower the seat to make it easier to touch the ground when stopped or dismounting.

Credit: Specialized Credit: Specialized

While other e-bikes I’ve owned initially offered good range, storing them in colder weather has been an issue for their longevity.

So I’m grateful that Specialized made the downtube battery removable. Being able to store the battery in my house helps since I keep my bikes in an outdoor shed—I no longer have to worry about it sitting in freezing temperatures. A full charge takes four hours, though you can tweak the battery to cap charging at 80 percent to further extend its life.

The other electric components are nice, too. On the Evo 5.0 I rode, a touchscreen lets you cycle through ride data, including your speed, estimated range remaining, and power assistance level. The front light even comes with low-beam (450 lumens) and high-beam (805 lumens) settings, making my night rides more visible.

If I have any complaints about my time with this bike, they boil down to two things. First, it’s incredibly heavy, making it difficult for many people to take up stairs. Second, the convenience of riding at high speed and making quick journeys has left my road bike gathering dust unless I’m specifically going out for a workout (and those are harder now that I’m so accustomed to riding the Evo). That’s more of a personal issue than a mark against the bike, though. It’s fun to ride, but maybe at the cost of consistent manual pedaling.

And then there’s the cost. I tested the Evo 5.0 ($5,200), which sits between the 4.0 ($4,500) and 6.0 ($7,000) in terms of cost, performance, and features. At $5,200, though, I would’ve liked to see a phone mount on the 5.0, which is a common enough feature on less-expensive bikes.

Those prices make securing the bike even more important, and Specialized offers a few safety features to thwart theft. As mentioned, you can remove the battery, lock the back wheel, and disable the power system; there’s also Apple Find My integration if the bike ever escapes your purview (a bummer if you use Android, though).

In all, Specialized has upgraded the e-bike experience enough for me to want to upgrade, though the price makes me blanch a bit. Maybe I can subsidize a purchase by selling an older e-bike.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Housing Starts Jump, But Drop in Permits Paints Dim Picture for Homebuilding

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Construction has seen a significant slowdown from continued market challenges in supply chain and affordability issues. Despite a surprising uptick in housing starts in this month’s New Residential Construction report, economists say the overall picture remains troubled.

The latest data from the Department of Housing and Urban Development and the Census Bureau saw housing starts jump 9.9% month-over-month to a rate of 1,309,000, also seeing a 2.8% year-over-year rise.

The rise was directly due to a leap in multifamily starts, up 25.5% month-over-month and 22% year-over-year to a rate of 433,000. Multifamily construction did see an uptick in building permits in September by 5.9% month-over-month and 1% year-over-year, which appears to have translated into the uptick in starts.

Single-family starts also saw a month-over-month rise of 4% to a rate of 864,000, but were still down year-over-year by 3.2%.

Notably, September’s original starts rate of 1,221,000 was negatively revised to 1,191,000, reflecting the continuation of the slower trend in construction. Single-family starts were also negatively revised from a rate of 859,000 to 831,000.

National Association of Home Builders (NAHB) Chairman Bill Owens said that the gain in starts this month is “encouraging,” but “does not necessarily signal a sustained recovery.”

“Builder confidence weakened in November as higher mortgage rates and affordability challenges weighed on buyers,” he continued. “Stronger demand is needed to sustain construction, while meeting the significant long-term need for more housing requires easing regulatory barriers to home building.”

Danushka Nanayakkara-Skillington—NAHB’s assistant VP for Forecasting and Analysis—agreed with Owens’ sentiment, adding that the gain is “welcome,” but the “market remains constrained by elevated borrowing costs, rising construction costs and weakening builder confidence.”

“The monthly gain should be viewed cautiously as affordability challenges and economic uncertainty persist,” she continued.

Looking regionally, the Midwest and the South were up month-over-month and year-over-year, while the Northeast and the West were down on both counts.

The Midwest was up 48.4% month-over-month and 17.2% year-over-year. The South was up 10.5% month-over-month and 12.2% year-over-year. The Northeast was down 20.8% month-over-month and 34.5% year-over-year. The West was down 2.6% month-over-month and 11.6% year-over-year.

Realtor.com® Senior Economist Joel Berner described the signals presented in this month’s report as “mixed,” and noted that single-family construction is still down year-over-year.

“This month’s data is an improvement, but from a very low baseline readout in both September and last October,” he continued.

Despite the rise in starts in October, building permits did not paint a picture of a continued upward trend.

Permits were down 7.4% month-over-month and 8.7% year-over-year to a rate of 1,295,000. Both multifamily and single-family permits were down month-over-month and year-over-year. Multifamily permits were down 5.2% month-over-month and 0.4% year-over-year to a rate of 475,000. Single-family permits were down 8.7% month-over-month and 12.9% year-over-year to a rate of 772,000.

Berner noted that this was permitting’s “lowest level since the early days of the COVID-19 pandemic in May 2020.” He also called this month’s permit data a “disappointment,” as it demonstrated that builders are “slowing down activity at the top of their funnel, which will result in slower months of starts and completions later on.”

Permits were also down both month-over-month and year-over-year in the Northeast, South and West, while the Midwest was up on both counts.

Housing completions had a more mixed result in October. Completions were up 6.5% month-over-month to a rate of 1,272,000, but were down 10.4% year-over-year. Both single-family and multifamily completions were also up month-over-month (6.3% and 9%, respectively), and were also down year-over-year (-10.7% and -9%, respectively).

“If you focus on home starts, this month’s new construction data is a welcome improvement from last month’s, but zooming out for more context shows a residential construction industry that is still struggling to find its footing,” concluded Berner. ”(S)oftening permits will put downward pressure on starts and completions in the months to come. In a country facing a supply gap of over four million homes, this represents a step in the wrong direction.”

Claudia Larsen is a senior editor for RISMedia.

The US Lost Its Measles Elimination Status. It’ll Take Years to Get It Back

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The US has hit terrible twin milestones over the past week: two more people died from measles, bringing this year’s death count to double digits, and the country officially lost its status as a country that had eliminated the highly infectious disease.

The US eliminated measles in 2000 and maintained that status for 26 years. Now, the nation faces an uphill battle to vanquish the virus once more, and it will take years to be measles-free again.

Noel Brewer, a professor of health behavior at the University of North Carolina, presented a report last week to the Pan American Health Organization showing continuous measles transmission for at least 12 months in the US—erasing one of the nation’s greatest public health achievements.

WIRED asked him how he felt after PAHO officials reached consensus the country was no longer measles-free. He paused.

“I have not actually thought about my reaction,” he says. After a beat, he says that the loss is “a somber moment for public health” that’s particularly wrenching because it’s a “completely avoidable situation.” Mexico and Canada have managed to wind down their outbreaks, but “measles in America is a runaway train,” Brewer says. “There's no end in sight, and it may even be gaining speed.”

PAHO director Jarbas Barbosa da Silva Jr. officially announced the change in status on Tuesday. “Losing a measles elimination status has broader implications than losing a public health designation,” he said at a press conference. “It means the virus has circulated for a prolonged period of time, putting families and communities at risk of preventable illness, hospitalization, and death.” Bolivia and Mexico also lost their elimination status this year; Canada lost it a year ago.

Brewer presented three key lines of evidence showing that measles has become endemic in the US. The outbreak in Utah and Arizona has gone on for more than 12 months, and the US has had measles cases every week since January 20, 2025. The genomic data also shows a great deal of similarity across states, indicating the clusters are closely linked—and each week, there are cases where the sources can’t be confirmed, indicating they didn’t come from travel or known contact with other patients.

“We have a problem on our hands,” says William Moss, epidemiology professor at Johns Hopkins. And it’s not going away anytime soon. To reclaim its status as measles-free, the US will have to prove it has gone a full year without continued transmission. That’s unlikely to happen under the Trump administration—and its actions have made it more difficult for future leaders.

“Reestablishing elimination is not just about reporting fewer cases—it also requires convincing the world that we have a strong enough surveillance system to find cases and deaths,” Jennifer Nuzzo, an epidemiologist and director of the Pandemic Center at Brown University, tells WIRED in a text.

The fact that the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and state health departments differ on how many people have died from measles “is a sign of dysfunction,” Nuzzo says. There have been 10 confirmed measles deaths so far this year—seven in Pennsylvania, two in Wisconsin, and one in New York—but the CDC only lists six.

Such chaos also breeds distrust, says Frederic Bertley, president and CEO of the Center of Science and Industry. That's “a huge problem” for a public trying to parse who is telling the truth and whether they should listen, he says.

Even as measles was eliminated, anti-vaccine rhetoric began to rise, especially around the measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine. Vaccination rates began falling—spurred in part by Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., an environmental lawyer turned anti-vaccine activist who published a now-retracted article alleging a link between vaccines and autism in 2005. He continued crusading against vaccines as head of Children’s Health Defense and at the helm of the Department of Health and Human Services during President Donald Trump’s second term.

The Trump administration has done little to stop the outbreaks, cutting billions in funding from state, local, territorial, and tribal public health departments and offering conflicting messages on vaccines. During a televised February 2025 cabinet meeting following the first child death in a decade, Kennedy said “it's not unusual, we have measles outbreaks every year.” The CDC hasn’t held a single press conference on measles throughout the entire outbreak; as PAHO was making its measles status announcement on Tuesday, HHS sent out an email about Kennedy’s cooking show.

“The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does,” CDC director Erica Schwartz said in a statement. “Losing a designation doesn't mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks.” Kennedy posted on X that “I continue to recommend MMR vaccination as the most effective way to prevent measles.”

To regain its elimination status, the US needs to address barriers to healthcare access, missed vaccination opportunities, misinformation, and vaccine hesitancy, Barbosa said. That includes mounting wide-reaching vaccination campaigns, expanding laboratory testing capacity, and responding quickly to cases, including contact tracing. It’s the bread and butter of public health, but the longer it takes to create a comprehensive response, the more it will cost, experts warn.

Future efforts to contain measles will require “a stronger centralized federal response to these outbreaks” paired with support for “very local responses,” says Moss. That means identifying communities with longstanding under-vaccination and understanding why, he says, as well as improving trust in public health guidance.

“Immunity gaps” tend to concentrate in specific geographic areas and communities, and national or even state-level data can “mask these significant inequalities, providing an incomplete picture of the population's actual level of protection,” Barbosa said.

The CDC announced $100 million in August and September—more than a year after the outbreak began—to help fund state and regional efforts to combat measles, but Congress will need to allocate $100 million to $400 million more for measles elimination in coming months, Brewer says.

“More than 90 percent of children get the measles vaccine, Brewer points out. “This is a vaccine that has extraordinary public support, and it goes across the aisle.”

But to achieve herd immunity, vaccination rates need to be at least 95 percent due to how contagious the disease is. High transmissibility also means measles is very hard to uproot once it begins to spread.

Yet ending national measles transmission is “its own reward,” Brewer says. It spares children’s lives, keeps them out of hospitals, prevents blindness and other lifelong complications. “We no longer have that benefit,” he says. “Children are now being stalked by this incurable and deadly disease.”

Mike Mills Spills R.E.M. Secrets, Sort of: Shirtless Recording and Much More

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

R.E.M. broke up in 2011, seemingly for good, but their story nearly ended even earlier than that. Making 1998’s Up, their first album without drummer Bill Berry, was a dark, fraught experience, and afterwards, they weren’t sure how to continue. But by the time the remaining members of R.E.M. — Michael Stipe, Mike Bills, and Peter Buck — made it back in the studio for 2001’s lush, sonically adventurous Reveal, they had reached a new equilibrium that would last a decade longer. With Reveal (25th Anniversary Edition) due Friday, Nov. 20, Mills joined Rolling Stone by Zoom to look back at the making of that album and much more. The new edition includes The Black Sessions Revealed, a previously unreleased live set that R.E.M. recorded for Radio France in Paris in 2001. (Those live performances are also getting their own double-LP release.) Mills is also keeping busy with his new supergroup, Howl Owl Howl (featuring Darius Rucker and founding Black Crowes drummer Steve Gorman), who just released a new song and are planning a tour next year.

The band has described Reveal as a summer record, a joyful record. But it’s not that joyful.No, it’s not. It’s more wistful. And the reason I see it as joyful is ’cause we had so much fun making it. We went to cities that we loved and worked with people that we loved, and we’d just gotten through all of the rough business of Bill leaving the band and trying to make Up without a drummer.

And so we were all just so happy to be done with all that and back on firm ground that to me, that’s the happiness of the record. Obviously, I listened to it a little while back and was like, it’s really not that happy. But we were happy. So it does capture, I think, the wistfulness of summer — fall is coming and you’ve got this free time, but it’s gonna end soon, and I don’t know, I think that’s in there as well.

It’s well documented that Up was painful, and you came out of it in a bummed-out place. How did you repair things enough to come into this record in such a positive place?We had some come-to-Jesus moments, and we had to make that decision: What do we care about here? What’s more important? Or is R.E.M. worth fighting through losing a band member over? And we decided it was. We had a great meeting out in the middle of nowhere and had a facilitator and just reaffirmed our commitment to what we were doing.

‘Cause we had to. We had to do something like that or we were just gonna be done after Up, and nobody wanted to end on Up. You wanna end on an up note, but not on Up. So we just recommitted and reconfirmed and went into Reveal with a really clear head space. Editor’s picks The 250 Greatest Albums of the 21st Century So Far The 100 Best TV Episodes of All Time The 500 Greatest Albums of All Time 100 Best Movies of the 21st Century

By a facilitator, you mean a therapist?I’m not gonna get into that, but we were at a point where we needed to do something drastic, so we did.

Did you see the documentary Some Kind of Monster, where Metallica had a therapist?No, but I’ve heard about it.

Maybe we can talk about the facilitator on the 30th anniversary. Will people ever stop asking whether you’ll reunite?Yeah, I’m sure when one of us kicks the bucket, it’ll stop. I don’t know how clear we can be. It’s like, I know that we got onstage with the Shannon-Narducy guys and Peter and I are in a couple of bands together and all of that stuff, but R.E.M. is done. R.E.M. as a recording and touring entity is over.

Short of a billboard every six months, I don’t know what else you can do.I’m glad people still care. Yeah. It’s good. I want our music to be in the zeitgeist, I want it to be heard and thought about and talked about and appreciated. And it’s also gratifying that people care enough about the band that they would like us to reform. That feels good, and I don’t ever wanna dismiss that sort of concern and care from our fans, but it’s not gonna happen.

Back to Reveal. I know it’s always been one of your favorites.It’s one of my favorites in the sense that I think our best records were cohesive, in the sense that they captured a feeling from the beginning to the end, which is what, to me, a record usually does. It captures the moment in time for the band when they were making it. And the more cohesive they are, the better feel you get for where the band was at the time. So Murmur, Automatic for the People, Reveal — I think they’re our most cohesive records, and I love them because of that. Related Content Coachella 2027 Lineup Takeaways: Madonna's Special Spot, Indie Surprises, and… No Drake? What's It Like to Write Songs With Madonna, McCartney, and the Stones? The Birth of ‘Confessions II’: Producer Stuart Price Breaks Down Madonna's Instant Classic Martin Garrix Set to Release ‘Debut’ Album, 13 Years in the Making

Like Automatic for the People, Reveal is in a quieter mode, and you’ve made some of your best records that way. How conscious was it when you decided, “We’re in that mode”?I don’t know that it was conscious. You write the songs you write because of whatever head space you’re in, even if you’re aware of it or not. And the approach, the production, the recording of that record was all done with — as I said, coming out of that period, the tough period before that — there was no sense of urgency. There was no sense of a sword of Damocles hanging over your head. There was no tour hanging over our head. A lot of times when you have a tour hanging, you think you gotta rock things up ’cause you’re gonna be playing them live. This one, we just weren’t worried about anything except sitting in those nice studios and making this record.

People reach for the Beach Boys as a comparison, but I’m not sure that’s what you were thinking, even on the lusher stuff.No, not at all. For example, I was definitely thinking Brian Wilson on “At My Most Beautiful” when we made Up. That was just 100 percent everything I could think of to wave at Brian and say, “Hey, we love you.” That was “At My Most Beautiful.” This record, no, not so much. There are some Sixties pop elements to it, but that’s ’cause we all grew up with Sixties pop and we love it. Wherever the song needed that, I’m more than happy to put it on there. But there was very little Beach Boys thought going on, or even Sixties thought going on during this time.

There’s a Jimmy Webb, “Wichita Lineman” feel to “Reno,” and a Walker Brothers or Bacharach lushness elsewhere. If your touchstones weren’t Brian Wilson, what were they?I think we were always careful about touchstones and things like that because that’s going to be in your thinking anyway, and if you consciously bring it up, then you might be a little more slavish to it than you intend to be. For us, I think it was more we’d put down what we would hear and then go, “Oh, that sounds like something Bryan Ferry might have done,” or whatever.

But no, we weren’t consciously thinking about that beforehand. It’s an after, post-facto thing rather than before.

As I understand it, you and Peter wrote the music, maybe 25 instrumental pieces to choose from. Were you writing in the same room, or bringing in half-finished demos for the other to add to?You know what, that’s a really good question. I think that since we weren’t living together like we used to do many years ago, or even living in the same town necessarily, we did a lot of separate writing, but we also wrote in the studio. There’s a lot of downtime in the studio, and Peter and I would sit there with guitars, and as a matter of fact, I think it’s “Chorus and the Ring.” There was a guitar riff I was working on and I couldn’t get anywhere with it, so I just put it aside, but apparently Peter had been listening and he took that riff and created this brilliant song out of it. That’s the kinda thing that you can’t plan for, but it happens.

So there are songs on the album that were mostly one person’s until the band got hold of them?Yeah. In our later iterations… Even at the beginning, it was like Lennon and McCartney in the sense that one person would write the verse and another one might write the chorus, and then who knows who would write the bridge. This stuff was a little less group-ish than that. It was definitely writing on your own and then bringing things in.

Reading the press from the time, and listening to the album, you were clearly determined not to repeat yourselves, or even to sound like R.E.M. Did that happen in the writing, or in choosing which pieces to use?That actually began some years earlier, probably right around Green [in 1988] or just after Green, because Green was Green and then of course whatever the next one is, and I’m blanking on the big one… Out of Time [in 1991].

A lot of that was us rejecting what we’d been doing because you’re like a shark. You gotta keep moving or you’ll drown. Peter didn’t wanna keep playing the same electric guitar, so we decided to start shaking things up, swapping instruments, and that worked for a couple of records. But then about that point, we also said, “OK, we can write R.E.M. songs, quote unquote, forever, but that stagnates, and we gotta keep it moving both for ourselves and for the fans.”

You have to keep it fresh. So yes, we rejected a lot of songs over the last few years around that time for sounding too much like R.E.M. We wouldn’t even give them to Michael half the time. We’d just say, “No, that’s a great song. Let’s put it in the shelf somewhere and maybe we’ll use it later.”

Did you kill songs in the cradle at home because they felt too familiar?That does happen, but at least in my case, it wasn’t because it sounded R.E.M. too much. I didn’t write as much as Peter did on this record, so I didn’t really have that issue. I don’t know what Peter thought while he was writing. He might’ve written 10 other things that he rejected because it sounded too much like R.E.M. “Imitation of Life” is on this record, right?

Yes, and I was gonna get to that.Yeah, that’s very R.E.M.-y, but, you can’t deny who you are. We’re not trying to pretend that we are not R.E.M. anymore. We love the same sounds that we loved before, and we love the same approach to songwriting that we did before.

You just try not to repeat yourself. And “Imitation of Life,” though, is such a great song, that it doesn’t matter that it sounds like an R.E.M. song. We are R.E.M., so why not?

At the time, you guys, and especially Peter, sounded almost resentful of that song. You talked about leaving it off the album. What do you remember about that?I think that may have been considered in the sense that we were trying to take a clear step in a different direction from the ’80s R.E.M. Even into the 2000s, we were still moving away, in our minds, from that. But on the other hand, it’s a great song, and you’d just be cutting off your nose to spite your face to say, “Oh no, that sounds too much like R.E.M., and out it goes.” That would’ve just been completely pointless and counterproductive.

Peter also sounded annoyed that everyone called it catchy, as if that were a dirty word. Do you remember that mentality in the band?No, but it makes sense. I don’t remember that, but it does make sense. It feels reductive. Yeah, it’s catchy. Great. It’s a pop song. Big deal. Believe me, we had plenty of dumb pop songs, and we were always happy to have ’em. But I don’t think there was any of that on this record and catchy, it’s just too easy. It’s like, we’re not trying to write catchy songs. We’re trying to write good, solid songs with some depth and meaning to them.

2001 was the year of the Strokes, but you guys seemed to be in your own world.We try to live in our world. Yeah. Especially making a record, because once you start, you have to be careful about the outside influences you let in. It can be very distracting. Yeah. And on the other hand, though, we’d go see a band and… Did we see the National around that time? I don’t know, but if you go see a band, they can —

Michael was very aware of Radiohead and Kid A.Yeah. And U2. We’ve always been very much in awe of what U2 did and very related to how they approach things. But more in the sense of just being inspired, not bringing this sound or that approach into it. It’s more like, “Oh, wow, that was a great band. Let’s go home and make a great song and a great record.” That’s really what we get from other people.

Speaking of U2: In 1993, you and Michael joined their rhythm section as Automatic Baby to play “One” at Clinton’s inaugural ball. What do you remember?Oh, that was a super fun night. Got to meet Chrissie Hynde. Had a little chat with her that night. That was fun. Getting to play with Adam and Larry was very cool. Getting to do that great song at a very happy time, it was such a positive, forward-looking time that it was just a thrill to be a part of it.

Right before Reveal came out, you decided it was too long and cut a longer song, “Fascinating.” Which brings up something else: You’ve chosen not to release demos or outtakes. Why?I don’t think we had enough for this record. I don’t think we had enough demos and outtakes. This was fairly focused. Yeah. The thing is, CDs were a curse and a blessing. With LPs, you had 40 minutes, give or take. With CDs, you could put 20 10-minute songs on there if you really wanted to. It gave a kind of freedom, but it also removed some of the boundaries that are good for bands when they’re making a record. We just tried to watch ourselves and not make it too long. You could put every song you like on there, but I think a 15-song record is just too long. We didn’t do it.

We finally got to hear “Fascinating” officially a few years ago and it leaked long before. It’s a great song. Yeah. It is a great song. But something had to go. Yeah. And that is usually a very hard decision to make, ’cause if you’ve gone to the trouble to write it, record it, produce it, get it ready to go, then you obviously consider it on a par with everything else that’s on the record, but something has to go. Maybe because it was slower, there are already enough slow songs on the record, I think that probably had something to do with it.

What do you remember about “All the Way to Reno” coming together?I loved that. When Peter brought that in I just said, “That’s beautiful, and that’s gonna work.” And I said, “Now I gotta come up with a bassline that matches that.” And then putting on the tremolo guitar just takes it into that Western zone that’s so nice. So yeah, I was really happy with that. I think that’s probably the high point of the record.

I really like “She Just Wants to Be.” Yeah. There’s just some strange songs on this record — again, rejecting the R.E.M. template of how to write songs, you have to come up with different approaches and different things, and that’s one thing I do like about this record, is there is an R.E.M. song, quote unquote, or two on there, but mostly it’s innovative for us.

I think you’ve said that “Beachball” is another one of your favorites.I really like “Beachball.” It’s kinda goofy. The string part I put on it is weird. There’s a part of it that’s kinda jarring. And I think [producer] Pat [McCarthy] said, “Are you sure you wanna do that?” I said, “Absolutely. That’s my favorite part of the whole thing.” ‘Cause it stays on the same half-step note longer than you feel like it might should have. But I really enjoy that. So I think “Beachball” is beautiful and wistful and just quirky enough to be good.

We don’t hear your voice much on Reveal, compared with other albums.It’s not on there much. And I’m really not sure why. It just worked out that way. If I hear something, I’ll sing it. But I just didn’t really feel the need to insert myself so much on this record. I don’t know, I don’t remember why that is, and I don’t remember thinking much about it at the time, but it is true. I don’t sing very much on it.

The live sessions on the reissue are fun: instant live versions that already rock the songs up a bit. How clearly do you remember that session?Oh, the Black Sessions? Yeah. Oh. That was just French TV, I think. Yeah. For the first 10 years, we were writing the songs and playing them live before we played them on the record. That really fine-tuned them. It rocked them up, because you wanna rock when you’re playing live. And then it got to a certain point where we were writing the songs, making the record, then playing them live.

And to your point, they would have rocked more had we played them live before we recorded them on the record. Going back to Up, “Walk Unafraid” live was just a straight balls-to-the-wall rocker. It was amazing, and that did not come across on the record.

Michael once said he was getting a lot of advice from Madonna on this record. Were you aware of that?I don’t, I do not know. Maybe through the ether, or maybe they were in contact more than I’m aware of. I don’t know. I don’t remember a lot of Madonna being bandied about. And I’m OK with that. I have a lot of respect for Madonna, but I don’t wanna make Madonna records.

I just talked to Andrew Watt about Michael’s solo album, which seems to have really picked up pace. Have you heard any of it?I have. He’s working his ass off on this thing. It sounds great. What I heard sounds really wonderful.

And you’ve got Howl Owl Howl, who just released another single, “Everybody Wants Her.” Did you finish the album?Yeah, the album is done. I’m running around like a chicken trying to get the artwork done. I’ve never done the artwork for a record before … I’m putting together the packaging for this one, and it’s not something I ever hoped to do, but hopefully it’ll turn out all right.

How does the rest of the record compare with what we’ve heard?“My Cologne” is a super power-pop song. There are a couple of others of those on the record, very similar in terms of approach to power-pop music. There are a couple of ballads. There’s a political song. There’s just some personal stuff. It’s more diverse than you would think just hearing that one song.

And you’re touring again?Yeah, we’re going out in early February. It’s a smaller tour ’cause Darius has a big summer tour with Keith Urban coming up, so we had to downsize this one a little bit, but it’s gonna be super fun.

Really good venues and some different cities. Louisville, Milwaukee, I think Memphis, fun place, Charleston. Just places we didn’t hit before.

I hope Howl Owl Howl comes back to New York.Not this tour, but hopefully one after that.

One last one from Reveal: “I’ll Take the Rain.” How did that one come together?That was one of the last ones in Miami. I remember going in, it was at night. I’d been to dinner, had a couple glasses of wine. I didn’t like the bass part, so I went in with Pat McCarthy and I took my shirt off, because I got that from Michael.

When Michael wanted to deliver kind of a sexy performance, he’d go in the vocal booth without his shirt. So I said, “OK, I’m gonna do that,” and I stood in the control room, not out in the studio. It was really dark and pretty colored lights everywhere and I just tried to get as into the song as I possibly could for the bassline. That was a really fun recording session. Trending Stories John Mulaney Enlists Stepfather-in-Law’s Country Band to Play ‘Kimmel’ 2026 CMA Awards: The Complete List of Winners Watch Buffalo Springfield Come Together for the First Time in Many Years Jennifer Lopez Teases New Music on ‘Fallon’: ‘This Does Feel Like a Happy Era’

Is that your only shirtless bassline?I think that’s probably the only shirtless bassline, yeah. Yeah.

We’ll have to listen for the difference.[Laughs.] Oh, you won’t. You’ll never know, but it’s there.

GTA 6 Is Already Breaking Twitch Records

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

The game’s streaming audience keeps growing as more regions get access

Grand Theft Auto 6 is already rewriting streaming history. Rockstar Games' highly anticipated sequel has attracted 3.29 million concurrent viewers on Twitch, breaking the platform's previous record for a video game launch as its global rollout continues.

According to preliminary data from Streams Charts, GTA 6 has now attracted a combined peak audience of 7,886,402 concurrent viewers across Twitch, YouTube, Kick, and other livestreaming platforms, at the time of this writing. Twitch accounts for the largest share, with a peak of 3.29 million viewers, followed by YouTube at 3.19 million and Kick at 1.48 million.

The latest surge came after GTA 6 became available in Singapore and Hong Kong, bringing another wave of players and content creators into the game. With its worldwide rollout still underway, those figures could climb even higher as additional regions gain access.

The combined audience puts GTA 6 comfortably ahead of every previous video game launch tracked by Streams Charts. Its closest competitor is Valorant, whose closed beta attracted 1.74 million concurrent viewers in 2020. Other major releases in the top 10 include Hogwarts Legacy (1.36 million), Path of Exile 2 (1.35 million), and Cyberpunk 2077 (1.32 million).

GTA 6 also become Twitch's most-watched gaming category with its peak of 3.29 million concurrent viewers. That puts it ahead of League of Legends, which previously held the record with 3.11 million viewers, and Fortnite, which peaked at 3.04 million.

Reviews for GTA 6 are also rolling in, and Rockstar is receiving near-universal critical acclaim ahead of the game's official launch. The game currently holds a 99 on Metacritic and a 98 on OpenCritic, with several outlets awarding perfect scores.

Polygon's review calls GTA 6 one of Rockstar's finest games to date, praising its astonishingly detailed open world, intricate gameplay systems, and the chemistry between protagonists Jason and Lucia. The review particularly highlights how the couple's heartfelt relationship gives the sprawling crime adventure an emotional center, while Leonida's reactive world makes exploration feel endlessly rewarding. There are some shortcomings, including a story that occasionally sags and a few performance dips, but the overall verdict is overwhelmingly positive.

With more regions still awaiting access, GTA 6 could establish an even larger streaming audience before its launch day is over.

Rockstar’s magnum opus searches for love and catharsis in desperate times — and finds them
